Logics with Multiteam Semantics Journal: TOCL CCS: Theory of computation Logic
Erich Grädel, Richard Wilke

Abstract
. Team semantics is the mathematical basis of modern logics of dependence and independence. In contrast to classical Tarski semantics, a formula is evaluated not for a single assignment of values to the free variables, but on a set of such assignments, called a team. Team semantics is appropriate for a purely logical understanding of dependency notions, where only the presence or absence of data matters, but based on sets, it does not take into account multiple occurrences of data values. It is therefore insufficient in scenarios where such multiplicities matter, in particular for reasoning about probabilities and statistical independencies. Therefore, an extension from teams to multiteams (i.e. multisets of assignments) has been proposed by several authors. In this paper we aim at a systematic development of logics of dependence and independence based on multiteam semantics. We study atomic dependency properties of finite multiteams and discuss the appropriate meaning of logical operators to extend the atomic dependencies to full-fledged logics for reasoning about dependence properties in a multiteam setting. We explore properties and expressive power of a wide spectrum of different multiteam logics and compare them to second-order logic and to logics with team semantics. In many cases the results resemble to what is known in team semantics, but there are also interesting differences. While in team semantics, the combination of inclusion and exclusion dependencies leads to a logic with the full power of both independence logic and existential second-order logic, independence properties of multiteams are not definable by any combination of properties that are downwards closed or union closed and thus strictly more powerful than inclusion-exclusion logic. We also study the relationship of logics with multiteam semantics with existential second-order logic for a specific class of metafinite structures. It turns out that inclusion-exclusion logic can be characterised in a precise sense by the Presburger fragment of this logic, but for capturing independence, we need to go beyond it and add some form of multiplication. Finally we also consider multiteams with weights in the reals and study the expressive power of formulae by means of topological properties.



1.
Introduction

Team semantics, originally invented by Hodges
(
Hodges97
)
to provide a compositional, model-theoretic semantics for
independence-friendly logic (IF)
(
MannSanSev11
)
, has become the mathematical basis of a wide variety of logics for reasoning
about dependence, independence, or imperfect information. In modern logics of this kind,
dependencies between variables are not presented anymore by annotations of quantifiers (as in IF logic)
but are considered, following a proposal by Väänänen
(
Vaananen07
)
, as atomic properties of teams.
A team is a set of assignments
s\colon\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}\to A

with the same domain of variables and the same co-domain of values, typically the universe of a structure.
Indeed, statements of the form “
y
depends on
x
” or “
x
and
y
are independent” do not make sense
for an individual assignment of values to
x
and
y
, but require a larger amount of data, which can be
modelled by a table or relation, or equivalently by a team of assignments.

Team semantics has turned out to be extremely fruitful and important for reasoning about dependence and independence.
It has lead to a genuinely new area in logic,
with an interdisciplinary motivation of providing logical systems for reasoning about
the fundamental notions of dependence and independence that permeate many scientific disciplines.
Methods from several areas of computer science, including finite model theory, database theory, and the algorithmic analysis of
games have turned out as highly relevant for this area. For more information, we refer to the
volume
(
DagstuhlDependenceBook
)
and the references therein.

However, one of the limitations of team semantics is that, based on sets,
it does not take into account multiplicities of assignments.
Team semantics thus provides a purely logical understanding of dependency notions,
where only the presence or absence of data matters.
For instance, the independence of two variables
x
and
y
in a team
X
means that
the values for
x
and
y
occur in all conceivable combinations in
X
or, equivalently,
that learning anything new about the actual value of one variable, does not provide any
additional information about the possible values for the other
(
GraedelVaa13
)
.
However, it may very well be that
x
and
y
are independent in this sense, but not in a statistical sense,
so that additional knowledge about
x
may lead to new insights into the
probabilities
for the possible values of
y
.
Team semantics is therefore insufficient in scenarios where multiplicities matter,
in particular for reasoning about probabilities and statistical independencies.
A natural semantical framework for logics of dependence and independence taking into account
multiplicities of data is obtained by considering
multisets
, rather than sets, of assignments.
This is also motivated by the prominent role of
bag semantics
in databases,
which is one of the areas in computer science where reasoning about dependencies
is an important issue
(
VardiCha93
;
JayramKolVee06
)
.
Indeed, the standard way of evaluating database queries in commercial systems
(like
SQL
) is based on bag semantics, and does not eliminate duplicates
in the query result, unless this is explicitly required by the user.
There are several reasons for this, one is that eliminating duplicates may be
computationally expensive and another one is that the multiplicities actually
affect the semantics, as for example when one queries which feature is the most
common among the present data.

Objectives of this paper

We aim at a systematic development of logics of dependence and independence based on
multiteam semantics. We study atomic dependency properties of finite multiteams and
discuss the appropriate meaning of logical operators for multiteam semantics, so as to extend
the atomic dependencies to full-fledged logics for reasoning about dependence and
independence in a multiteam setting.
We compare the properties and
expressive power of a number of different logics with team and multiteam semantics,
and show that in some important aspects, multiteam semantics is quite different from team semantics.
For instance, while in team semantics, the combination of inclusion and exclusion dependencies leads to
a logic with the full power of both independence logic and existential second-order logic,
this is not the case for logics with multiteam semantics. In this
setting independence is not definable by any combination of properties
that are downwards closed or union closed and thus strictly more powerful
than inclusion-exclusion logic.
We shall also study the relationship of logics with multiteam semantics with
metafinite model theory, more precisely, with existential second-order
logic for a specific class of metafinite structures. It turns out that inclusion-exclusion logic
can be characterised in a precise sense by the Presburger fragment of this logic, but
for capturing independence, one would need to go beyond it and add some form of multiplication.
Finally, we shall study multiteams with weights in the real numbers, which leads to the notions of topologically
open and closed formulae. Topological properties can be used
to separate certain variants of multiteam logics with respect to expressive power.
Interestingly, this also provides additional insights into the connections between
team semantics and multiteam semantics with natural multiplicities.

Related work

The need and the potential of generalising team semantics by taking into account multiplicities
have been noted before, and several approaches for specific applications have been proposed.
Hyttinen et al.
(
HyttinenPaoVaa15
)
have introduced quantum team logic, a propositional logic for reasoning about quantum phenomena,
and have provided a semantics based on a specific notion of multiteams, called quantum teams.
In a further paper, the same authors
(
HyttinenPaoVaa17
)
have introduce measure teams, and a logic for probabilities
of first-order properties of these.

A closely related approach to multiteam semantics is the study of
probabilistic teams

where the individual assignments come with a probability rather than a multiplicity.
Durand et al.
(
DurandHanKonMeiVir18a
)
study probabilistic team semantics with
rational probabilities and compare the power of different logics in this setting.
Probabilistic teams have been further studied in
(
HannulaHirKonKulVir19
)
, with probabilities that are not
confined to the rational numbers. Both papers also make connections to variants of existential
second-order logic, that are somewhat similar to the ones established here, although our setting and our
methods are different. Probabilistic team semantics is an important research direction,
which clearly has similarities to multiteam semantics, but also relevant differences.
Probabilistic notions for teams abstract away from the concrete multiplicities of data and
instead focus exclusively on relative probabilities, whereas in our work
the actual multiplicities are taken into account, which in many application scenarios, such as
databases, is of importance.
We believe that both approaches, probabilistic team semantics and multiteam semantics,
are worth investigating in detail and we here contribute to the latter case.

Multiteams as such have been considered by Durand et at. in
(
DurandHanKonMeiVir18
)
,
but in a different setting where not only the data (team) is equipped with multiplicities, but also the elements of
the underlying structure, so that also the universe is a multiset, rather than a set.
Here instead, we have a purely logical
view on the structure and attach multiplicities to the data records only, i.e. to the
assignments in a multiteam. The differences of the two approaches also lead to different
definitions for the meaning of logical operators.
In
(
DurandHanKonMeiVir18
)
the authors study a property of multiteam formulae
that they call
weak flatness

which basically means that a formula holds under a multiteam if, and only if, it holds
under a multiteam based on the same team, but where all assignments occur just once,
and they show that weak flatness is a property shared by formulae
in which only downwards closed dependencies occur.
A multiteam in which every assignment occurs at most once may be considered as a
team, hence this result builds a bridge between team and multiteam semantics.
In the present paper we also consider links between these two formalisms,
but instead of identifying a fragment of multiteam formulae which in their
meaning coincide with team semantical formulae we study the question which properties
that are expressible in a logic with team semantics can also be expressed
in multiteam semantics (when one has access only to multiteam atoms and not to team
atoms, of course).
A further matter, which is addressed in
(
DurandHanKonMeiVir18
)
and in a slightly different form by
Väänänen in
(
Vaananen17
)
, is the notion
of
approximate operators

\left<p\right>\vartheta
and
[p]\vartheta
, where

p
is a probability.
These formulae are satisfied by a multiteam
M
if some/all submultiteams
N
of

M
with size at least
p\cdot|M|
satisfy
\varphi
.
Although we do not investigate these operators in this paper, we will introduce
a variety of atomic multiteam formulae, most notably the
forking atoms

{\bar{x}}\sphericalangle_{\leq p}{\bar{y}}
with which it is possible to define the existential
approximate operators in our setting.
Further, Hirvonen et al.
(
HirvonenKonPau19
)
study multiteams from a different angle, considering open and closed formulae based on
metric spaces.

2.
Team Semantics

We recall the standard definitions related to team semantics (see, e.g.,
(
DagstuhlDependenceBook
;
Galliani12
;
GraedelVaa13
;
Vaananen07
)
).
A
team

X
is a set of assignments
s\colon D\to A
with a common finite domain
D=\dom(X)

of variables and a common codomain
A
of values, typically the universe of a structure
\mathfrak{A}
.
For a team
X
with domain
\dom(X)
and
V\subseteq\dom(X)
the
restriction

of
X
to
V
is the team
X{\upharpoonright}_{V}:=\{s{\upharpoonright}_{V}:s\in X\}
.
We write
s[x\mapsto a]
for the assignment
that extends, or updates,
s
by mapping
x
to
a
.
We write
\mathcal{P}(A)
for the power set of
A
and
set
\mathcal{P}^{+}(A):=\mathcal{P}(A)\setminus\{\varnothing\}
.
Basic operations that possibly extend
the domain of a given team to new variables are the unrestricted

generalisation
over
A
,

X[x\mapsto A]:=\{s[x\mapsto a]:s\in X,a\in A\}

and the
Skolem-extensions

X[x\mapsto F]:=\{s[x\mapsto a]:s\in X,a\in F(s)\}

for any function
F\colon X\to\mathcal{P}^{+}(A)
.
Modern logics of dependence and independence are based on atomic properties of teams, that are then extended to full-fledged
logics for reasoning about such dependencies, using the rules of team semantics.

1 (Atomic dependencies).

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a structure and
X
a team over
\mathfrak{A}
.

Dependence::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\operatorname{dep}({\bar{x}},y)\ \mathbin{:\!\!\iff}\ (\forall s,s^{\prime}\in X)\ s({\bar{x}})=s^{\prime}({\bar{x}})\Rightarrow s(y)=s^{\prime}(y)
;
(
Vaananen07
)

Exclusion::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\operatorname{|}}{\bar{y}}\ \mathbin{:\!\!\iff}\ (\forall s,s^{\prime}\in X)\ s({\bar{x}})\neq s^{\prime}({\bar{y}})
;
(
Galliani12
)

Inclusion::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}{\bar{x}}\subseteq{\bar{y}}\ \mathbin{:\!\!\iff}\ (\forall s\in X)(\exists s^{\prime}\in X)\ s({\bar{x}})=s^{\prime}({\bar{y}})
;
(
Galliani12
)

Equiextension::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}{\bar{x}}\Join{\bar{y}}\ \mathbin{:\!\!\iff}\ \mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}{\bar{x}}\subseteq{\bar{y}}
and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}{\bar{y}}\subseteq{\bar{x}}
;
(
Galliani12
)

Anonymity::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}{\bar{x}}\Upsilon y\ \mathbin{:\!\!\iff}(\forall s\in X)(\exists s^{\prime}\in X)\ s({\bar{x}})=s^{\prime}({\bar{x}})
and
s(y)\neq s^{\prime}(y)
;
(
DagstuhlDeplog19
)

Independence::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}{\bar{x}}\perp{\bar{y}}\ \mathbin{:\!\!\iff}\ (\forall s,s^{\prime}\in X)(\exists s^{\prime\prime}\in X)\ s^{\prime\prime}({\bar{x}})=s({\bar{x}}),s^{\prime\prime}({\bar{y}})=s^{\prime}({\bar{y}})
.
(
GraedelVaa13
)

An important issue in the study of atomic dependencies are
closure properties
. Dependence
and exclusion atoms are
downwards closed
: whenever such a dependency is true for a team
X
,
then it is also true for all subteams
Y\subseteq X
. Similarly, inclusion, equiextension, and anonymity atoms are

closed under unions
: whenever they are true in all teams
X_{i}
(for
i\in I
), then they
also hold in their union
\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
. Independence atoms instead are neither downwards closed nor
closed under unions.

Given any collection
\Omega
of atomic team properties, we denote by
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
the closure
of the atoms from
\Omega
and the first-order literals (of some fixed relational vocabulary
\tau
)
under the logical operators
\land,\lor,\exists
, and
\forall
.

The traditional semantics (to which we refer as Tarski semantics) for
\varphi\in\mathrm{FO}

is based on single assignments
s
whose domain must comprise the variables
in
\free(\varphi)
; we write
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}\varphi[s]
for saying that
\mathfrak{A}
satisfies

\varphi
with the assignment
s
.
The rules of team semantics extend the semantics of the atomic dependencies to tell us when it is the case that

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\varphi
for
\varphi(\bar{x})\in{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
, a
\tau
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
and a team
X

whose domain includes the free variables of
\varphi
.

2 (Team Semantics).

•

If
\varphi
is a literal then
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\varphi
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}\varphi[s]
for all
s\in X
;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\varphi_{i}
for
i=1,2
;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
if
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}
for two teams
X_{i}
such that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X_{i}}\varphi_{i}
;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\forall x\varphi
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X[x\mapsto A]}\varphi
;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\exists x\varphi
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X[x\mapsto F]}\varphi
for some
F\colon X\to\mathcal{P}^{+}(A)
.

Besides this standard variant for team semantics (sometimes called
lax
semantics), there also exists
a
strict
variant, which for disjunctions requires a
disjoint
decomposition
X=X_{1}\hbox to13.33pt{\vbox to12.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.66635pt\lower-6.1108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.33334pt}{-2.77779pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.38889pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}X_{2}
,
and for existential quantification a Skolem extension
X[x\mapsto F]
with
F\colon X\to A
,
which selects for each assignment
s\in X
a single value for the quantified variable, rather than a non-empty set of values.
The distinction between the two variants is immaterial for logics that are downwards closed (such as
exclusion or dependence logic) but for inclusion logic or independence logic, the
strict semantics is deficient in the sense that it violates the
locality principle

that a formula should only depend on the variables occurring in it.
We shall see that this is different for the multiteam semantics developed below;
there a strict variant of the semantics is the ‘right’ one.

One way to understand the expressive power of a logic with team semantics is to relate it
to some well-understood logic with classical Tarski semantics.
One can translate formulae
\varphi(\bar{x})
from a logic
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
with vocabulary
\tau

into
sentences

\varphi^{X}
, with Tarski semantics, of
vocabulary
\tau\,\hbox to13.33pt{\vbox to12.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.66635pt\lower-6.1108pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.33334pt}{-2.77779pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.38889pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\{X\}
where
X
is an additional relation symbol for the team, such that
for every structure
\mathfrak{A}
and every team
X
we have that

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\varphi(\bar{x})\ \Longleftrightarrow\ (\mathfrak{A},X)\mathbin{\vDash}\varphi^{X},

where
(\mathfrak{A},X)
is the expansion of
\mathfrak{A}
by a relational encoding of the team
X
.
In all logics with team semantics that extend first-order formulae
by atomic dependencies that are themselves first-order definable,
and which do not make use of additional
connectives beyond
\land,\lor
and atomic negation
(in particular no connective with which one can define classical negation),
such a translation will always produce sentences
in (a fragment of) existential second-order logic
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
.
Understanding the expressive
power of a logic
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
with team semantics thus means to identify the
fragment of
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
to which
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
is equivalent in the sense just described.
By now, many results of this kind are known; some of the most important ones are the following.

•

Dependence logic
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\operatorname{dep}]
and exclusion logic
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[{}\mathbin{\operatorname{|}}{}]
are equivalent to the fragment of
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
-sentences
\psi(X)
in which the predicate for the team appears only negatively
(
KontinenVaa09
)
.

•

Inclusion logic is equivalent to sentences of form
\forall\bar{x}(X\bar{x}\rightarrow\psi(X,\bar{x}))
in the posGFP-fragment of least fixed-point logic, such that
X
occurs only positively in
\psi(X,{\bar{x}})

(
GallianiHel13
)
.

•

Independence logic
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\perp]
and inclusion-exclusion logic
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\subseteq,{}\mathbin{\operatorname{|}}{}]
are equivalent with full
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
(and thus can describe all NP-properties of teams)
(
Galliani12
)
.

•

{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\cup\text{-game}]
is equivalent to the fragment of all union closed formulae of
\operatorname{\Sigma_{1}^{1}}

(
HoelzelWil20
)
.

3.
Atomic Dependencies for Multiteams

Multisets and multiteams

A
multiset
(or bag)
M
is a pair
(S,m)
where
S
is a set and
m\colon S\to\mathbb{N}_{>0}

describes the multiplicities of the elements
s\in S
in
M
. For
x\notin S
we define
m(x)\mathbin{:=}0
.
Given two function
m\colon S\to\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and
m^{\prime}\colon S^{\prime}\to\mathbb{N}_{>0}
and a binary operator

\circ\colon\mathbb{N}_{>0}\times\mathbb{N}_{>0}\mapsto\mathbb{N}_{>0}
put
m\circ m^{\prime}\colon S\cup S^{\prime}\to\mathbb{N}_{>0},s\mapsto m(s)\circ m^{\prime}(s)
.
The size of
M
is
|M|\mathbin{:=}\sum_{s\in S}m(s)
and the
additive union

with
M^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},m^{\prime})
is
M\uplus M^{\prime}:=(S\cup S^{\prime},m+m^{\prime})
.
Their
product
is
M\times N:=(S\times S^{\prime},m\cdot m^{\prime})
.
Further,
M
is a
submultiset
of
M^{\prime}
,
in symbols
M\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}M^{\prime}
, if
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
and
m(s)\leq m^{\prime}(s)
for all
s\in S
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
kM:=(S,k\cdot m)
, where
0M
is the empty multiteam
(\varnothing,\varnothing)
.
In case we explicitly present a multiset by listing its elements, we use double set braces as in
\{\mkern-5.0mu\{a,a,b\}\mkern-5.0mu\}=(\{a,b\},a\mapsto 2,b\mapsto 1)
.
We also make use of the notation
\{\mkern-5.0mu\{x\in M:\rho(x)\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
,
where
M
is a multiset, to denote the restriction of
M
to those elements

x
that satisfy the condition
\rho
.
Formally, this is the multiset
\biguplus_{x\in M,\rho(x)}m(x)\cdot\{\mkern-5.0mu\{x\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.

A
multiteam
is a multiset
M=(X,m)
where
X
is a team.
We write
T(M)
for the underlying team
X
of
M
.
The domain and codomain of
M
are those of
T(M)
and the restriction of
M

to the domain
V
is the multiteam
(T(M){\upharpoonright}_{V},m^{\prime})
, where
m^{\prime}\colon s\mapsto\sum_{s^{\prime}\in T(M),s^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}_{V}=s}m(s^{\prime})
.
In this paper we only consider finite multiteams
M
, with
|M|\in\mathbb{N}
, and we
tacitly assume this whenever we talk about multiteams.
The multiset of values of a variable tuple
{\bar{x}}
in a multiteam
M
is

M({\bar{x}}):=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s(\bar{x}):s\in M\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.
We denote the restriction of
M
to those assignments that map
{\bar{x}}
to
{\bar{a}}
by

M_{{\bar{x}}={\bar{a}}}:=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s\in M:s({\bar{x}})={\bar{a}}\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.
For any first-order atom
\beta
we define
the restriction of a multiteam
M
to all assignments that satisfy
this formula, by putting
M_{\beta}:=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s\in M:\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}\beta[s]\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
, where
the structure
\mathfrak{A}
should be clear in the given context.
For a tuple
{\bar{a}}
of elements from
A
, the
probability
that a randomly chosen
assignment of the multiteam
M
maps
{\bar{x}}
to
{\bar{a}}
is
\pr_{M}({\bar{x}}={\bar{a}}):=|M_{{\bar{x}}={\bar{a}}}|\,/\,|M|
.
Further, we have the conditional probabilities
\pr_{M}({\bar{x}}={\bar{a}}\mid{\bar{y}}={\bar{b}}):=|M_{{\bar{x}}{\bar{y}}={\bar{a}}{\bar{b}}}|\,/\,|M_{{\bar{y}}={\bar{b}}}|
(which is undefined in case
M_{{\bar{y}}={\bar{b}}}
is empty).

1.

Let
M=(X,m)
be a multiteam with values in
A
. The universal extension of
M
on a variable
x
is

M[x\mapsto A]\mathbin{:=}(X[x\mapsto A],m^{\prime})
where
m^{\prime}(s[x\mapsto a]):=m(s)
.
Equivalently, we can write
M[x\mapsto A]=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s[x\mapsto a]:s\in M,a\in A\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.
A choice function
F\colon M\to A
for
x
on
M
assigns to every (occurrence of an)
assignment
s
in
M
a value in
A
;
different occurrences of the same assignment can be mapped to distinct elements.
The Skolem extension
M[x\mapsto F]
is the multiteam of all assignments
s[x\mapsto a]

extending (occurrences of )
s
in
M
by the values assigned by
F
.
Formally,
F
is a function whose domain is the set of all tuples
(s,i)
satisfying

s\in T(M)
and
0\leq i<m(s)
, with codomain
A
, which we indicate by writing

F\colon M\to A
as an abbreviation.
Accordingly,
M[x\mapsto F]
is the multiteam
\biguplus_{s\in T(M),i<m(s)}\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s[x\mapsto F(s,i)]\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.
Notice that
|M[x\mapsto F]|=|M|
for every multiteam
M
and every choice function
F
, whereas for universal extensions, we have

|M[x\mapsto A]|=|M|\cdot|A|
.

Atomic dependencies

Of course, we can view any atomic dependency property
\alpha
for teams also as a property for multiteams
by defining that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{T(M)}\alpha
.
In fact, for notions such as dependence and exclusion (which are downwards closed statement that do not
refer to multiplicities), this is the natural way to go.
However, for properties such as inclusion, independence or forking, we
work with different definitions that take multiplicities into account and
are appropriate for statistical dependencies of data.
The following atomic properties capture relevant dependencies for multiteams.

Multiteam inclusion::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Forking::

For
\vartriangleleft\in\{<,\leq,=,\geq,>\}
and
p\in[0,1]
we define

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}{\bar{x}}\sphericalangle_{\vartriangleleft p}{\bar{y}}
, if, and only if, for all
{\bar{a}},{\bar{b}}\in A^{*}
where
\pr_{M}({\bar{y}}={\bar{b}}\mid{\bar{x}}={\bar{a}})>0
also
\pr_{M}({\bar{y}}={\bar{b}}\mid{\bar{x}}={\bar{a}})\vartriangleleft p
holds.

Statistical independence::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}{\bar{y}}
holds if, and only if,
\pr_{M}({\bar{x}}={\bar{a}})=\pr_{M}({\bar{x}}={\bar{a}}\mid{\bar{y}}={\bar{b}})
for all
{\bar{a}}\in M({\bar{x}})
and

{\bar{b}}\in M({\bar{y}})
.
An equivalent condition is
M({\bar{x}})\times M({\bar{y}})=|M|\cdot M({\bar{x}}{\bar{y}})
.

Conditional independence::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}_{\bar{z}}{\bar{y}}
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M_{{\bar{z}}={\bar{c}}}}{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}{\bar{y}}
holds for all
{\bar{c}}\in A^{|{\bar{z}}|}
.

Forking atoms have also been considered in the setting of team semantics
(
GraedelHeg16
)
.

One can come up with many other atomic dependencies for multiteams.
There is one relevant condition that we shall impose for such dependencies
\alpha

which is
invariance under multiplication
of data. This means that
for every structure
\mathfrak{A}
and every multiteam
M
we have

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{kM}\alpha
for every natural number
k>0
.
Clearly, all dependencies that we have defined satisfy this condition. If it is abandoned, one could define
pathological atoms (e.g. one saying that
|M|
is prime or
|M|<5
) that would violate some of the
properties of multiteam semantics that we are going to prove in this paper.

Downwards closed atoms

A multiteam atom
\alpha
is
downwards closed
if

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
and
N\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}M
imply that also
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{N}\varphi
.
Important examples are dependence and exclusion which are taken over from team semantics.
We show next that, in fact, all downwards closed atoms only depend on the underlying team
and are, in this sense, really team semantical atoms.
We shall establish a stronger result in Section
6
.

Proposition 2.

Let
\alpha
be a downwards closed atom for multiteams.
Then for all multiteams
M,N
with
T(M)=T(N)
we have that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{N}\alpha
.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
then, by closure under multiplication, also
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{|N|\cdot M}\alpha
.
But if
N
and
M
are based on the same supporting team, then
N\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}|N|\cdot M
, so by downwards closure,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{N}\alpha
.
∎

Union closed atoms

Also the notion of union closure naturally extends to multiteams. We call

\alpha

union closed
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{N}\alpha
imply that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M\uplus N}\alpha
.

(1)

The atoms
{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

,
{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(5)

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

if, and only if,

can be decomposed into (finitely many) multiteams

such that
M=\biguplus_{i}M_{i}
, every multiplicity occurring in an

is 1 and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M_{i}}{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\bar{y}}

Proof.

Claim (1) is easily checked. For Claim (2) we notice that for any tuples
{\bar{x}}
and
{\bar{y}}
of the same length, we have

|M({\bar{x}})|=|M({\bar{y}})|
in every multiteam
M
.
If
{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\bar{y}}
holds in
M
then, for every value
{\bar{a}}
we have that
|M_{{\bar{x}}={\bar{a}}}|\leq|M_{{\bar{y}}={\bar{a}}}
|.
In fact we then have that
|M_{{\bar{x}}={\bar{a}}}|=|M_{{\bar{y}}={\bar{a}}}|
, because otherwise there must exist some other value

{\bar{b}}
with
|M_{{\bar{x}}={\bar{b}}}|>|M_{{\bar{y}}={\bar{b}}}|
, contradicting the assumption that
{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

4.
Multiteam Semantics for First-Order Logic

In the same way as team semantics generalises from a set
\Omega
of atomic dependencies for teams
to logics
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
we would also like to have full-fledged logics for reasoning about
dependency properties of multiteams. Syntactically, a collection
\Omega
of atomic
dependencies of
multiteams
is extended to logics
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}[\Omega]
in a completely
analogous way, by closing the atoms from
\Omega
and the first-order literals (of some vocabulary
\tau
),
by
\lor,\land,\exists
and
\forall
, thus insisting that formulae are in negation normal form, and that
negation is applied only to first-order atoms, not to atomic dependencies.

The multiteam semantics of first-order literals is as in the case of team semantics, i.e. for every structure
\mathfrak{A}
, every multiteam
M
, and every
first-order literal
\alpha(\bar{x})
we define that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}\alpha[s]
for all
s\in M
.
However, to come up with appropriate definitions for the multiteam semantics of the logical operators
is not completely trivial and
we have to justify the choices that we make. To do this we shall formulate certain
postulates

that an appropriate multiteam semantics should satisfy, and we shall argue
that our choices are essentially enforced by these postulates. Of course, the postulates themselves cannot be ‘proved’;
however, we hope to convince the readers that they reflect natural properties that should be satisfied by
reasonable semantical rules, and that violating them leads to pathological or undesired effects.

4.1.
Postulates and definitions for multiteam semantics

The first postulate is the
locality principle
, familiar from team semantics,
that the meaning of a formula should depend only on those variables that occur free in it.

The second postulate states that multiteam semantics should be, in a reasonable sense, compatible with team semantics.
Since every atom for teams can also be used as an atom for multiteams, we have for every collection
\Omega

of team semantical atoms, a natural embedding of
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
in
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}[\Omega]
.
We postulate that every
\varphi\in{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
that holds for a team
X
also holds
for
some
multiteam with base set
X
and vice versa.
The reasoning behind this postulate is that multiteam semantics provides a more
detailed view on the data than team semantics, hence, if a statement holds under
multiteam semantics it should
a fortiori
hold under plain team semantics.
On the other hand, knowing that a purely team semantical property is satisfied by

X
it should be possible to provide adequate multiplicities to the assignments
in
X
such that the same property holds under multiteam semantics.

The third postulate concerns disjunctions; even in team semantics, disjunction is in general not idempotent, and a well-known example
witnessing this is the constancy atom
\operatorname{const}(x):=\operatorname{dep}(\varnothing,x)
. Indeed, while
\operatorname{const}(x)
is true in a team
X
if, and only if,
the value of
x
is constant in
X
, the formula
\operatorname{const}(x)\lor\operatorname{const}(x)
holds in
X
if, and only if,
x
takes at most two values
in
X
. A necessary and sufficient condition for
\varphi\equiv\varphi\lor\varphi
is that

\varphi
is
closed under (finite) unions
. We postulate that this equivalence should also hold in multiteam semantics.

Our final postulate concerns a natural property of quantifiers: they should not
affect atomic dependencies that do not involve the quantified variable.

Here is the list of our postulates:

P1: Locality.:

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M{\upharpoonright}_{\free{\varphi}}}\varphi
.

P2: Compatibility with team semantics.:

For any team-semantical formula
\varphi\in{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Omega]
we have that

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{X}\varphi
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{(X,n)}\varphi
for some
n\colon X\to\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.

P3: Idempotence of disjunction.:

\varphi\equiv\varphi\lor\varphi
if, and only if,
\varphi
is
union closed
.

P4: Dummy quantification preserves atomic dependencies.:

For both
Q=\exists
and
Q=\forall
we have that
Qx\,\alpha\equiv\alpha
for
all multiteam atoms
\alpha
in which the variable
x
does not occur.

Based on these postulates we define multiteam semantics by the following rules.

1.

Let
\Omega
be a collection of multiteam dependency atoms,
\mathfrak{A}
a structure,
M
a multiteam over
A
and

\varphi,\psi\in{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}[\Omega]
.

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\alpha
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}\alpha[s]
for all
s\in M
if
\alpha
is a first-order literal;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi\land\psi
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi
and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\psi
;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi\lor\psi
if there are
M_{1}\uplus M_{2}=M
with
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M_{1}}\varphi
and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M_{2}}\psi
;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\forall x\psi
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M[x\mapsto A]}\psi
;

•

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\exists x\psi
if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M[x\mapsto F]}\psi
for some choice function
F\colon M\to A
.

By induction one can show that first-order formulae under multiteam
semantics are
flat
, that means
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi
if, and only if,

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s\}\mkern-5.0mu\}}\varphi
for all
s\in T(M)
.
Further
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s\}\mkern-5.0mu\}}\varphi
holds precisely if
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}\varphi[s]

under Tarski semantics, hence we generalise the notation
M_{\varphi}
to arbitrary
first-order formulae, not just to atoms, with the meaning
M_{\varphi}=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s\in M:\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}\varphi[s]\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.

Classical negation is not part of our syntax.
But since negation is allowed in front of classical first-order atoms and the
dual of every operator is present, first-order formulae without dependencies can
always be transformed into negation normal form.
For first-order
\zeta
, we let
\zeta\rightarrow\chi

be a shorthand for
\operatorname{nnf}(\neg\zeta)\lor(\zeta\land\chi)
.
Note that
\zeta
occurs on both sides of the disjunction to ensure that precisely
those assignments are evaluated against
\chi
that satisfy
\zeta
.
One can check that this implication satisfies
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\zeta\rightarrow\chi

if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M_{\zeta}}\chi
.
It would mean something different if we would have defined it to be equivalent
to
\operatorname{nnf}(\neg\zeta)\lor\chi
, since then we would only have that
the implication is satisfied by
\mathfrak{A}
and
M
in case there is a multiteam
N

which is sandwiched between
M_{\zeta}
and
M
, i.e.
M_{\zeta}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

4.2.
The complexity of evaluation in multiteam semantics

It is often useful to describe the evaluation process for determining
whether
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\psi
by an
annotation
of the syntax tree
\mathcal{T}(\psi)

of
\psi
, which associates with every subformula
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}(\psi)
a multiteam
M(\varphi)
.
We call such an annotation
structurally valid
(for
\mathfrak{A},M
, and
\psi
) if
M(\psi)=M
and
the annotation respects the semantic rules of Definition
1
as follows:

•

M(\varphi\land\vartheta)=M(\varphi)=M(\vartheta)
;
M(\varphi\lor\vartheta)=M(\varphi)\uplus M(\vartheta)
;

•

for
\varphi=\forall x\vartheta
, we have that
M(\vartheta)=M(\varphi)[x\mapsto A]
, and

•

for
\varphi=\exists x\vartheta
, we have that
M(\vartheta)=M(\varphi)[x\mapsto F]
for some choice function
F\colon M\to A
.

Proposition 4.

For all
\mathfrak{A},M
, and
\psi
, we have that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\psi
if, and only if, there exists a structurally valid annotation of the syntax tree
\mathcal{T}(\psi)
, such that, for every leaf
\varphi
of
\mathcal{T}(\psi)
(which is either a first-order literal, or an atomic dependency) we have that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M(\varphi)}\varphi
.

For complexity analysis, we encode multiteams
M=(X,m)
via binary representations of the numbers
m(s)
, for
s\in X
.
We observe that the data complexity of
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}
is in
NP
, since for any fixed formula, annotations of polynomial size can be guessed and efficiently verified.
Further, it is well-known that even
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\operatorname{dep}]
can express
NP
-complete problems
(
Vaananen07
)
.

Corollary 5.

Let
\Omega
be any collection of multiteam atoms that can be evaluated in polynomial time on any given
\mathfrak{A}
and
M
.
Then, for every
\psi\in{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}[\Omega]
, the problem whether
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\psi
for given
\mathfrak{A}
and
M
, can be solved in
NP
. Further, whenever
\Omega
contains
\operatorname{dep}
,
\mid
, or
\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}
, then
there are formulae in
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}[\Omega]
, for which the evaluation problem is
NP
-complete.

Proof.

By Proposition
4
, we can determine whether
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\psi
by guessing an annotation
of the syntax tree
\mathcal{T}(\psi)
and verifying that it is structurally valid and satisfies the atoms. Since
\psi
is fixed,
so is the syntax tree. Going from a node of
\mathcal{T}(\psi)
to its successors can only decrease the multiplicities in the multiteams.
In particular, the multiplicity of an assignment
s[x\mapsto a]
in
M(\varphi)
is not larger than the multiplicity
of
s
in
M(\exists x\varphi)
or
M(\forall x\varphi)
.
Since the number of variables in subformulae of
\psi
is bounded by a constant,
an annotation of
\mathcal{T}(\psi)
can be represented with polynomially bounded length, with respect to the
length of the inputs
\mathfrak{A}
and
M
, and clearly the verification that
an annotation is structurally valid and satisfies the atoms can be done in polynomial time. This proves that the evaluation problem
for any fixed formula
\psi\in{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}[\Omega]
is in
NP
.
Finally since there even are team semantical formulae describing
NP
-complete problems, this holds
a fortiori

for
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{M}}}[\Omega]
, when
\Omega
contains, for instance dependency atoms
\operatorname{dep}(x,y)
.
∎

4.3.
Justification of the semantical rules

For the semantics of first-order atoms, conjunction and universal quantification
there are no reasonable alternatives to the semantics defined above,
in particular when keeping in mind our second postulate about compatibility with team semantics.
However, for the existential operators, i.e. disjunction and existential quantification,
there are other options that one has to consider when defining the semantics.
We elaborate on these and justify the choices we made in the following paragraphs.

Disjunction

As in team semantics, the disjunction must (again due to postulate two) in some
way allow to split the multiteam into two parts each of which satisfies one of
the disjuncts.
There are two possibilities: the first one
just requires that the two parts cover the given multiteam,
but permits to put an individual assignment into both parts.

(
\lor
-cover)

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi\lor\psi\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S}\varphi\text{~and~}\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{T}\psi\quad\text{for some~}S,T\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}M\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}S\uplus T

The other possibility is to require a disjoint split of the
multiteam into two parts.

(
\lor
-split)

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi\lor\psi\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S}\varphi\text{~and~}\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{T}\psi\quad\text{for some~}S,T\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}M=S\uplus T

The choice between the two possibilities
is irrelevant for downwards closed formulae since for these the two variants
(

∨

-cover
) and (

∨

-split
)
lead to equivalent meanings for the disjunction. However, for formulae that are not downwards closed,
such as inclusion and independence statements, it does make a difference.
Recall that in team semantics there is a similar distinction between
the strict and lax semantics for disjunction
(
Galliani12
)
. There it turns out that
the lax semantics, allowing splits that are not disjoint, is the right
choice, because otherwise the locality postulate is violated for certain formulae.
We argue that for multiteam semantics the situation
is different, and a disjoint split is the good choice.
On an intuitive level, if data is modelled by teams then
information about multiplicities of data is omitted, and it may be necessary to
assume that the data must be present in both parts of a split.
But in multiteams, information about multiplicities is available,
and if we recombine two parts of a multiteam then we have the sum of their multiplicities,
so it is much more natural to allow each assignment to be contained in precisely
one of the parts of a splits.
On a more formal level we justify the choice for (
\lor
-split)
by the postulate of equivalence between union closure and idempotent disjunction.

6.

Let
\varphi
be a formula with multiteam semantics. Modelling disjunctions via (
\lor
-split) we have that

\varphi\equiv\varphi\lor\varphi
if, and only if,
\varphi
is union closed.

Proof.

Every formula
\varphi
implies
\varphi\lor\varphi
because of the empty multiteam property.
Assume that
\varphi
is union closed.
Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a structure and
M
a multiteam such that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi\lor\varphi
.
Thus there is a split
M=S\uplus T
with
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S}\varphi
and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{T}\varphi
.
By union closure we obtain
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S\uplus T}\varphi
.
For the other direction assume that
\varphi\equiv\varphi\lor\varphi
and that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S}\varphi
and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{T}\varphi
hold.
Therefore
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S\uplus T}\varphi\lor\varphi
and hence
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S\uplus T}\varphi
.
∎

The following example illustrates that this postulate is violated if we interpret
disjunction by (
\lor
-cover).

7.

Let
\varphi=x\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

.

and

, which would imply that

\{0,1\}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi\lor\varphi
.

Existential Quantification

To define the semantics of a formula
\exists x\varphi
on a multiteam
M

(and a structure
\mathfrak{A}
) we have to
provide an adequate definition of how
M
should be modified by values for
the quantified variable
x
to obtain a multiteam that satisfies
\varphi
.
Thus the definition should have the form that

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\exists x\varphi
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M[x\mapsto F]}\varphi

for some function
F\colon D\to C
.

The domain
D
of
F
can either be the multiteam
M

(so that
F
can assign different values for
x
to
different occurrences of an assignment in
M
) or
its underlying team
T(M)
(so that all occurrences of
an assignment get the same value for
x
).
Also for the codomain
C
of
F
there are several possible choices:
It can either be the universe
A
of
\mathfrak{A}
(so that
for each assignment we assign precisely one value to
x
), which is our preferred choice,
or the set
\mathcal{P}^{+}(A)
(which, as in team semantics, means that
we update each assignment by a non-empty set of values for
x
).
Further, we could even assume that
F
assigns to each
s\in M
a multiset of
k
values for
x
,
for some fixed positive
k
(constant for all assignments).
This last possibility is of a probabilistic flavour, similar to the setting of probabilistic team semantics

(
DurandHanKonMeiVir18
)
or
(
HyttinenPaoVaa15
)
, and removes, for instance, the information on
how many assignments had originally been present. This is not the right choice for the setting
multiteam semantics intended to argue with multiplicities, and would also not be in line with
our definition for the disjunction, which is not probabilistic in nature.
A further problem with such a choice concerns the algorithmics of evaluation:
it is not clear at all that a similar result to Corollary

5
would hold if the existential quantifier could increase the size of
multiteams arbitrarily.

Altogether this gives four remaining possible definitions but it turns out that
only one of these is compatible with our postulates.
Taking the codomain
C=\mathcal{P}^{+}(A)
, which is the right choice
in team semantics, leads in multiteam semantics to a violation of
the postulate that a dummy quantification of a variable that is not present in
an atomic dependence should not change its meaning.
To put it differently, this principle means that for a multiteam
M
with domain
\mathcal{V}

and a variable
x\not\in\mathcal{V}
the multiteam

M[x\mapsto F]{\upharpoonright}_{\mathcal{V}}
satisfies the same atomic dependencies as
M
does.
However, if we permit the sets
F(s)
to be arbitrary non-empty subsets of
A
,
then these may have different sizes, so the multiteam

M[x\mapsto F]{\upharpoonright}_{\mathcal{V}}
may have completely different relative multiplicities
of its assignments, and therefore different atomic dependence properties.
As an example, consider
M=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s_{0}\colon xy\mapsto 01,s_{1}\colon xy\mapsto 10\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
and
F\colon s_{0}\mapsto\{0\},s_{1}\mapsto\{0,1\}
.
Since
M=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s_{0},s_{1}\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
and
T(M)=\{s_{0},s_{1}\}
it is irrelevant for
this particular example how we define the domain of our choice functions.
We have
\{0,1\}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}x\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}y
but
\{0,1\}\mathbin{\nvDash}_{M[z\mapsto F]}x\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}y
.
These considerations show that the function
F
must assign to each occurrence of an assignment
s
precisely one element of
A
.

It remains to show that a choice function
F
should have the
liberty to assign different values for
x
to
different occurrences of an assignment in
M
.
For this let
\mathfrak{A}=\{a,b\}
and
M=2\cdot\{\mkern-5.0mu\{x\mapsto a\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
and consider the
formula
\varphi\mathbin{:=}\exists y\forall z(xz\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}xy)
. It states, for
\mathfrak{A}
and
M
, that we can assign to
y
values such that
for all values
a
of
x
(which in our case is just one) the multiset
M[y\mapsto F]{\upharpoonright}_{x=a}(y)
is a multiple of
A
(cf. Proposition
3
).
For a choice function that can assign different values to different occurrences of an assignment, we certainly have that
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\varphi
.
But if we forbid this liberty then this would not be the case.
However, consider the multiteam
M^{\prime}=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s_{1}\colon xu\mapsto aa,s_{2}\colon xu\mapsto ab\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.
In this case, the restricted choice function
G\colon s_{1}\mapsto a,s_{2}\mapsto b
satisfies
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M^{\prime}[y\mapsto G]}\forall z(xz\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}xy)
although
M=M^{\prime}{\upharpoonright}_{x}
.
Thus, admitting only restricted choice functions, that must assign the same value to each occurrence of
an assignments would lead to a violation of the locality principle.

Universal versus existential quantification

Finally, we discuss a possible postulate that we had considered at some point, but then decided to discard,
and that is not satisfied by the multiteam semantics that we propose here. In most common
logics it is obviously true that
\forall x\varphi\mathbin{\vDash}\exists x\varphi
.
However, we do not think that it is justified to demand this as a postulate in our context, because of the
different roles that these quantifiers have in the setting of teams and multiteams,
compared to classical Tarskian settings. Classically, a universal quantifier can be thought of
as giving an opponent the power to
choose
an element that makes ‘proving’
the statement at hand as difficult as possible, whereas an existential quantifier gives
this choosing power to the proponent, so that she can select an element that makes the
‘proof’ as simple as possible. However, in a setting of teams and multiteams, a universal quantifier does
not represent a choice, but a
uniquely defined extension
of the current semantic object that
involves all elements of the given structure. The existential quantifier instead does indeed represent
a choice. Therefore we see in this context no specific reason to impose that a universal quantification should
generally imply an existential one. Of course there can still be settings where this is the case, such as in
lax team semantic, because the unique universal extension of a team happens to be also a Skolem extension,
or in probabilistic team semantics.
However, the implication
\forall x\varphi\mathbin{\vDash}\exists x\varphi
does not hold, for instance, in strict team semantics.
In our setting, the universal quantifier increases the size of a multiteam while the existential one (as defined here) does not.
One example of a formula
\varphi
for which
\forall x\varphi\mathbin{\vDash}\exists x\varphi
fails, is
\varphi(x):=\sphericalangle_{\leq 1/2}x
.
Indeed,
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{\{\mkern-5.0mu\{\varnothing\}\mkern-5.0mu\}}\forall x\;\sphericalangle_{\leq 1/2}x
holds for all structures with at least two elements,
but
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\nvDash}_{\{\mkern-5.0mu\{\varnothing\}\mkern-5.0mu\}}\exists x\;\sphericalangle_{\leq 1/2}x
.

5.
Comparison of logics with different multiteam dependencies

As in team semantics, the logical operators
preserve
certain fundamental properties of formulae,
in the sense that whenever both
\psi
and
\varphi
have that property, then so do

\psi\lor\varphi
,
\psi\land\varphi
,
\exists x\psi
and
\forall x\psi
. Examples of such properties are:

Downwards closure::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\psi
implies
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{N}\psi
for all
N\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}M
.

Team semantical downwards closure::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{(X,m)}\psi
implies
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{(Y,m{\upharpoonright}_{Y})}\psi
for all
Y\subseteq X
.

Union closure::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{R}\psi
and
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{S}\psi
implies
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{R\uplus S}\psi
.

Closure under scalar multiplication::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{M}\psi
implies
\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{kM}\psi
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Validity in the empty multiteam::

\mathfrak{A}\mathbin{\vDash}_{(\varnothing,\varnothing)}\psi
.

From the closure properties of atomic multititeam dependencies, we thus obtain the following closure properties for logics with
multiteam semantics.

All formulae in

are downwards closed.

{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}\precneqq{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\operatorname{dep}]\equiv{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[{}\mathbin{\operatorname{|}}{}]\precneqq{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\subseteq,{}\mathbin{\operatorname{|}}{}]\equiv{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}]\equiv{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\mathbin{\perp\!\!\!\perp}_{c}]
.

•

{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}\precneqq{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\Upsilon]\equiv{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\subseteq]\precneqq{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\subseteq,{}\mathbin{\operatorname{|}}{}]
. Further
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[\subseteq]
and
{\mathrm{FO}^{\mathsf{T}}}[{}\mathbin{\operatorname{|}}{}]
are incomparable.

We would like to understand the relative expressive power of logics with different multiset dependencies in a similar way.
Some of the results from team semantics carry over (with minor modification of the proofs) to multiteam semantics.

Proof.

The team semantical translations between exclusion and dependence logic from
(
Galliani12
)

work in multiteam semantics as well.
Translations proving the claims made for forking atoms can be found in
(
Wilke20
)
.
For the different variants of inclusion, observe that
{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.8889pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.41667pt}{-1.9618pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}

Let us explain the last equivalence. For a multiteam
M
on variables
{\bar{x}}
and
{\bar{y}}
, let

M_{\alpha}=\{\mkern-5.0mu\{s\in M:M\mathbin{\vDash}\alpha[s]\}\mkern-5.0mu\}
.
The formula
{\bar{x}}\mathbin{\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to8.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.41667pt\lower-4.05971pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
